wry (FA, \par}

\noindent\textbf{Q.2} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries A layer-4 firewall (a device that can look at all protocol headers up to the transport layer) CANNOT \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(A)}~block entire HTTP traffic during 9:00PM and 5:00AM\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(B)}~block all ICMP traffic\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(C)}~stop incoming traffic from a specific IP address but allow outgoing traffic to the same IP \newline address\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(D)}~block TCP traffic from a specific user on a multi-user system during 9:00PM and 5:00AM.
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.2} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Ts, Ay (Fucaca «) where c; and c? are constants
The database can be configured to do ordered indexing on A, or hashing on A, - Which of the
following statements is TRUE? \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(A)}~Ordered indexing will always outperform hashing for both queries\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(B)}~Hashing will always outperform ordered indexing for both queries\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(C)}~Hashing will outperform ordered indexing on Q1, but not on Q2\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(D)}~Hashing will outperform ordered indexing on Q2, but not on QI \newline  \newline 2011 cs

\noindent\textbf{Q.3} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries If two fair coins are flipped and at least one of the outcomes is known to be a head, what is the
probability that both outcomes are heads? \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular*}{\columnwidth}{@{\extracolsep{\fill}}p{0.46\columnwidth}p{0.46\columnwidth}@{}}

\addlinespace[2pt]
\textbf{(C)}~1/2 & \textbf{(D)}~2/3

\noindent\textbf{Q.4} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Consider different activities related to email.
ml: Send an email from a mail client to a mail server
m2: Download an email from mailbox server to a mail client
m3: Checking email in a web browser
Which is the application level protocol used in each activity? \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(A)}~ml: HTTP m2:SMTP\_ m3: POP\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(B)}~ml: SMTP m2: FTP m3: HTTP\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(C)}~ml: SMTP m2: POP m3: HTTP\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(D)}~ml: POP m2: SMTP m3: IMAP
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.6} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Let the time taken to switch between user and kernel modes of execution be t; while the time taken
to switch between two processes be tf. Which of the following is TRUE? \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(A)}~1) >t \newline B)t=h \newline (O)ti<t\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(D)}~nothing can be said about the relation between tf, and f2 \newline CS-A 220 \newline 2011 cs
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.7} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries A company needs to develop digital signal processing software for one of its newest inventions. The
software is expected to have 40000 lines of code. The company needs to determine the effort in
person-months needed to develop this software using the basic COCOMO model. The multiplicative
factor for this model is given as 2.8 for the software development on embedded systems, while the
exponentiation factor is given as 1.20. What is the estimated effort in person -months? \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular*}{\columnwidth}{@{\extracolsep{\fill}}p{0.46\columnwidth}p{0.46\columnwidth}@{}}
\textbf{(A)}~234.25 & \textbf{(B)}~932.50 \\
\addlinespace[2pt]
\textbf{(C)}~287.80 & \textbf{(D)}~122.40
\end{tabular*}
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.8} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Which of the following pairs have DIFFERENT expressive power? \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(A)}~Embed web objects from different sites into the same page\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(B)}~Refresh the page automatically after a specified interval\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(C)}~Automatically redirect to another page upon download\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(D)}~Display the client time as part of the page
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.10} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}

document? \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(A)}~Functional Requirements\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent

\noindent\textbf{Q.11} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Acomputer handles several interrupt sources of which the following are relevant for this question.
¢ Interrupt from CPU temperature sensor (raises interrupt if CPU temperature is too high)
¢ Interrupt from Mouse (raises interrupt if the mouse is moved or a button is pressed)
© Interrupt from Keyboard (raises interrupt when a key is pressed or released)
¢ Interrupt from Hard Disk (raises interrupt when a disk read is completed)
Which one of these will be handled at the HIGHEST priority? \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(A)}~Interrupt from Hard Disk\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(B)}~Interrupt from Mouse\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(C)}~Interrupt from Keyboard\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(D)}~Interrupt from CPU temperature sensor \newline CS-A 3/20 \newline 2011 cs
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}

\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent

\textbf{(C)}~UID is a candidate key if all students are from the same country\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(D)}~p>

\noindent\textbf{Q.16} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries A thread is usually defined as a ``light weight process'' because an operating system (OS) maintains
smaller data structures for a thread than for a process. In relation to this, which of the following is
TRUE? \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(A)}~On per-thread basis, the OS maintains only CPU register state\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(B)}~The OS does not maintain a separate stack for each thread\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(C)}~On per-thread basis, the OS does not maintain virtual memory state\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(D)}~On per-thread basis, the OS maintains only scheduling and accounting information \newline CS-A 4/20 \newline 2011 cs
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}

\noindent\textbf{Q.28} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Ona non-pipelined sequential processor, a program segment, which is a part of the interrupt service
routine, is given to transfer 500 bytes from an I/O device to memory.
Initialize the address register
Initialize the count to 500
LOOP: Load a byte from device
Store in memory at address given by address register
Increment the address register
Decrement the count
If count $\ne$ 0 go to LOOP
Assume that each statement in this program is equivalent to a machine instruction which takes one
clock cycle to execute if it is a non-load/store instruction. The load-store instructions take two clock
cycles to execute.
The designer of the system also has an alternate approach of using the DMA controller to
implement the same transfer. The DMA controller requires 20 clock cycles for initialization and
other overheads. Each DMA transfer cycle takes two clock cycles to transfer one byte of data from
the device to the memory.
What is the approximate speedup when the DMA controller based design is used in place of the
interrupt driven program based input-output? \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular*}{\columnwidth}{@{\extracolsep{\fill}}p{0.46\columnwidth}p{0.46\columnwidth}@{}}
\textbf{(A)}~3.4 & \textbf{(B)}~4.4 \\
\addlinespace[2pt]
\textbf{(C)}~5.1 & \textbf{(D)}~6.7
\end{tabular*}
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}

\noindent\textbf{Q.32} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Consider a database table T containing two columns X and Y each of type integer. After the

at any point in time. Using MX and MY, new records are inserted in the table 128 times with X and
Y values being MX+1, 2*MY+1 respectively. It may be noted that each time after the insertion,
values of MX and MY change.

out?
SELECT Y FROM T WHERE X=7; \par}

\textbf{(B)}~255 (©) 129\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(D)}~257

\subsection*{OFFICIAL ANSWER KEY --- GATE 2011 (CS)}
\vspace{-4pt}

\noindent\textbf{Q.1} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Consider the following logical inferences.
\par\smallskip
I1: If it rains then the cricket match will not be played.
The cricket match was played.
Inference: There was no rain.
I2: If it rains then the cricket match will not be played.
It did not rain.
Inference: The cricket match was played.
\par\smallskip
Which of the following is TRUE? \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(A)}~Both I1 and I2 are correct inferences\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(B)}~I1 is correct but I2 is not a correct inference\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(C)}~I1 is not correct but I2 is a correct inference\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(D)}~Both I1 and I2 are not correct inferences
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.2} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Which of the following is TRUE? \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(A)}~Every relation in 3NF is also in BCNF\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(B)}~A relation R is in 3NF if every non-prime attribute of R is fully functionally dependent on every \newline  \newline key of R\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(C)}~Every relation in BCNF is also in 3NF\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(D)}~No relation can be in both BCNF and 3NF
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.3} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries What will be the output of the following C program segment?
\par\smallskip
char inChar = `A' ;
switch ( inChar ) \{
case `A' : printf (``Choice A\ n'') ;
case `B' :
case `C' : printf (``Choice B'') ;
case `D' :
case `E' :
default : printf ( `` No Choice'' ) ; \} \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(A)}~No Choice\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(B)}~Choice A\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(C)}~Choice A \newline Choice B No Choice\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(D)}~Program gives no output as it is erroneous
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}

\textbf{(B)}~NP-complete $\cap$ P = \par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(C)}~NP-hard = NP\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(D)}~P = NP-complete
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}

\noindent\textbf{Q.27} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Consider the following transactions with data items P and Q initialized to zero:
\par\smallskip
T1 :read (P);
read (Q);
if P = 0 then Q := Q + 1 ;
write (Q).
\par\smallskip
T2 : read (Q);
read (P);
if Q = 0 then P := P + 1 ;
write (P).
\par\smallskip
Any non-serial interleaving of T1 and T2 for concurrent execution leads to \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(A)}~a serializable schedule\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(B)}~a schedule that is not conflict serializable\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(C)}~a conflict serializable schedule\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(D)}~a schedule for which a precedence graph cannot be drawn
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}

\noindent\textbf{Q.32} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Fetch\_And\_Add(X,i) is an atomic Read-Modify-Write instruction that reads the value of
memory location X, increments it by the value i, and returns the old value of X. It is used in the
pseudocode shown below to implement a busy-wait lock. L is an unsigned integer shared variable
initialized to 0. The value of 0 corresponds to lock being available, while any non-zero value
corresponds to the lock being not available.
\par\smallskip
AcquireLock(L)\{
while (Fetch\_And\_Add(L,1))
L = 1;
\}
\par\smallskip
ReleaseLock(L)\{
L = 0;
\}
\par\smallskip
This implementation \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(A)}~fails as L can overflow\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(B)}~fails as L can take on a non-zero value when the lock is actually available\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(C)}~works correctly but may starve some processes\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(D)}~works correctly without starvation

\noindent\textbf{Q.33} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}

\noindent\textbf{Q.47} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries The height of a tree is defined as the number of edges on the longest path in the tree. The function
shown in the pseudocode below is invoked as height(root) to compute the height of a binary
tree rooted at the tree pointer root.
\par\smallskip
int height (treeptr n)
\{ if (n == NULL) return -1;
if (n $\to$ left == NULL)
if (n $\to$ right == NULL) return 0;
\par\smallskip
else return ; // Box 1
\par\smallskip
else \{ h1 = height (n $\to$ left);
if (n $\to$ right == NULL) return (1+h1);
else \{ h2 = height (n $\to$ right);
\par\smallskip
return ; // Box 2
\}
\}
\}
\par\smallskip
The appropriate expressions for the two boxes B1 and B2 are \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(A)}~B1: (1+height(n $\to$ right)) \newline B2: (1+max(h1, h2))\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(B)}~B1: (height(n $\to$ right)) \newline B2: (1+max(h1,h2))\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(C)}~B1: height(n $\to$ right) \newline B2: max(h1, h2)\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(D)}~B1: (1+ height(n $\to$ right)) \newline B2: max(h1, h2) \newline B1 \newline B2 \newline 2012 COMPUTER SCIENCE \& INFORMATION TECH. -- CS \newline  \newline Common Data Questions \newline Common Data for Questions 48 and 49: \newline  \newline Consider the following C code segment. \newline int a, b, c = 0; \newline  \newline void prtFun(void); \newline main( ) \newline \{ \newline static int a = 1; /* Line 1 */ \newline  \newline prtFun( ); \newline  \newline a += 1; \newline  \newline prtFun( ); \newline  \newline printf(`` \n \%d \%d '', a, b); \newline  \newline \} \newline void prtFun(void) \newline \{ \newline static int a = 2; /* Line 2 */ \newline  \newline int b = 1; \newline a += ++b; \newline  \newline printf(`` \n \%d \%d '', a, b); \newline  \newline \}

\noindent\textbf{Q.63} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries An automobile plant contracted to buy shock absorbers from two suppliers X and Y. X supplies
60\% and Y supplies 40\% of the shock absorbers. All shock absorbers are subjected to a quality test.
The ones that pass the quality test are considered reliable. Of X's shock absorbers, 96\% are reliable.
Of Y's shock absorbers, 72\% are reliable.
\par\smallskip
The probability that a randomly chosen shock absorber, which is found to be reliable, is made by Y
is \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent

\noindent\textbf{Q.10} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries A scheduling algorithm assigns priority proportional to the waiting time of a process. Every process
starts with priority zero (the lowest priority). The scheduler re-evaluates the process priorities every
T time units and decides the next process to schedule. Which one of the following is TRUE if the
processes have no I/O operations and all arrive at time zero? \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(A)}~This algorithm is equivalent to the first-come-first-serve algorithm.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(B)}~This algorithm is equivalent to the round-robin algorithm.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(C)}~This algorithm is equivalent to the shortest-job-first algorithm.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(D)}~This algorithm is equivalent to the shortest-remaining-time-first algorithm.
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}

\end{tabular*}

\noindent\textbf{Q.13} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Using public key cryptography, X adds a digital signature $\sigma$ to message M, encrypts <M, $\sigma$>, and
sends it to Y, where it is decrypted. Which one of the following sequences of keys is used for the
operations? \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(A)}~Encryption: X's private key followed by Y's private key; Decryption: X's public key followed \newline by Y's public key\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(B)}~Encryption: X's private key followed by Y's public key; Decryption: X's public key followed \newline by Y's private key\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(C)}~Encryption: X's public key followed by Y's private key; Decryption: Y's public key followed \newline by X's private key\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(D)}~Encryption: X's private key followed by Y's public key; Decryption: Y's private key followed \newline by X's public key \newline 2013 COMPUTER SCIENCE \& INFORMATION TECH. - CS
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.14} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Assume that source S and destination D are connected through two intermediate routers labeled R.
Determine how many times each packet has to visit the network layer and the data link layer during
a transmission from S to D. \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(A)}~Network layer -- 4 times and Data link layer -- 4 times\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(B)}~Network layer -- 4 times and Data link layer -- 3 times\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(C)}~Network layer -- 4 times and Data link layer -- 6 times\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(D)}~Network layer -- 2 times and Data link layer -- 6 times
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.15} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries An index is clustered, if \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(A)}~it is on a set of fields that form a candidate key.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(B)}~it is on a set of fields that include the primary key.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(C)}~the data records of the file are organized in the same order as the data entries of the index.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(D)}~the data records of the file are organized not in the same order as the data entries of the index.
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.16} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Three concurrent processes X, Y, and Z execute three different code segments that access and
update certain shared variables. Process X executes the P operation (i.e., wait) on semaphores
a, b and c; process Y executes the P operation on semaphores b, c and d; process Z executes the
P operation on semaphores c, d, and a before entering the respective code segments. After
completing the execution of its code segment, each process invokes the V operation (i.e., signal) on
its three semaphores. All semaphores are binary semaphores initialized to one. Which one of the
following represents a deadlock-free order of invoking the P operations by the processes? \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(A)}~X: P(a)P(b)P(c) Y: P(b)P(c)P(d) Z: P(c)P(d)P(a)\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(B)}~X: P(b)P(a)P(c) Y: P(b)P(c)P(d) Z: P(a)P(c)P(d)\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(C)}~X: P(b)P(a)P(c) Y: P(c)P(b)P(d) Z: P(a)P(c)P(d)\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent

\noindent\textbf{Q.34} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}

memory, and then terminates. Each of the processes Y and Z reads x from memory, decrements by
two, stores it to memory, and then terminates. Each process before reading x invokes the P
operation (i.e., wait) on a counting semaphore S and invokes the V operation (i.e., signal) on the
semaphore S after storing x to memory. Semaphore S is initialized to two. What is the maximum
possible value of x after all processes complete execution? \par}

\textbf{(B)}~--1\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(C)}~1\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(D)}~2 \newline 2013 COMPUTER SCIENCE \& INFORMATION TECH. - CS

FROM Students as S, Registration as R
WHERE R.rollno=S.rollno AND R.courseno=107 AND R.percent >90
(II) ∏sname($\sigma$courseno=107 ∧ percent>90 (Registration ⋈ Students))
(III) \{T | ∃S$\in$Students, ∃R$\in$Registration ( S.rollno=R.rollno ∧
R.courseno=107 ∧ R.percent>90 ∧T.sname=S.sname)\}
(IV) \{<SN> | ∃SR∃RP ( <SR, SN>$\in$Students ∧ <SR, 107, RP>$\in$Registration ∧ RP>90)\} \par}

{\bfseries A certain computation generates two arrays a and b such that a[i]=f(i)for 0 $\le$ i < n and
b[i] = g (a[i] )for 0 $\le$ i < n. Suppose this computation is decomposed into two concurrent processes
X and Y such that X computes the array a and Y computes the array b. The processes employ two
binary semaphores R and S, both initialized to zero. The array a is shared by the two processes. The
structures of the processes are shown below.
Process X:
private i;
for (i=0; i<n; i++) \{
a[i] = f(i);
ExitX(R, S);
\}
Process Y:
private i;
for (i=0; i<n; i++) \{
EntryY(R, S);
b[i] = g(a[i]);
\}
Which one of the following represents the CORRECT implementations of ExitX and EntryY? \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(A)}~ExitX(R, S) \{ \newline P(R); \newline V(S); \newline \} \newline EntryY(R, S) \{ \newline P(S); \newline V(R); \newline \}\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(B)}~ExitX(R, S) \{ \newline V(R); \newline V(S); \newline \} \newline EntryY(R, S) \{ \newline P(R); \newline P(S); \newline \}\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(C)}~ExitX(R, S) \{ \newline P(S); \newline V(R); \newline \} \newline EntryY(R, S) \{ \newline V(S); \newline P(R); \newline \}\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(D)}~ExitX(R, S) \{ \newline V(R); \newline P(S); \newline \} \newline EntryY(R, S) \{ \newline V(S); \newline P(R); \newline \}

{\bfseries What is the return value of f(p,p), if the value of p is initialized to 5 before the call? Note that
the first parameter is passed by reference, whereas the second parameter is passed by value.
int f (int \&x, int c) \{
c = c - 1;
if (c==0) return 1;
x = x + 1;
return f(x,c) * x;
\} \par}

\}

\noindent\textbf{Q.63} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries After several defeats in wars, Robert Bruce went in exile and wanted to commit suicide. Just before
committing suicide, he came across a spider attempting tirelessly to have its net. Time and again,
the spider failed but that did not deter it to refrain from making attempts. Such attempts by the
spider made Bruce curious. Thus, Bruce started observing the near-impossible goal of the spider to
have the net. Ultimately, the spider succeeded in having its net despite several failures. Such act of
the spider encouraged Bruce not to commit suicide. And then, Bruce went back again and won
many a battle, and the rest is history.
Which one of the following assertions is best supported by the above information? \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(A)}~Failure is the pillar of success.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(B)}~Honesty is the best policy.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(C)}~Life begins and ends with adventures.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(D)}~No adversity justifies giving up hope.

possibility? \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(A)}~Neither ܮ nor ܮത is recursively enumerable (r.e.).\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(B)}~One of ܮ and ܮത is r.e. but not recursive; the other is not r.e.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(C)}~Both ܮ and ܮത are r.e. but not recursive.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(D)}~Both ܮ and ܮത are recursive.
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.36} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Which of the regular expressions given below represent the following DFA?
I) 0*1(1+00*1)*
II) 0*1*1+11*0*1
III) (0+1)*1 \par}

\addlinespace[2pt]
\textbf{(C)}~II and III only & \textbf{(D)}~I, II, and III
\end{tabular*}

respectively from bags 1 to 5. Their total weight comes out to 323 gm. Then the product of the
labels of the bags having 11 gm coins is \_\_\_.

1

1

\noindent\textbf{Q.40} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[NAT]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Consider the following function
\par\smallskip
double f(double x)\{
if( abs(x*x -- 3) < 0.01) return x;
else return f(x/2 + 1.5/x);
\}
\par\smallskip
Give a value q (to 2 decimals) such that f(q) will return q:\_\_\_\_\_. \par}
\par\vspace{3pt}\noindent\textbf{Answer:} \rule{3.2cm}{0.4pt}
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.41} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Consider the following C function in which size is the number of elements in the array E:
\par\smallskip
int MyX(int *E, unsigned int size)
\{
int Y = 0;
int Z;
int i, j, k;
\par\smallskip
for(i = 0; i < size; i++)
Y = Y + E[i];
\par\smallskip
for(i = 0; i < size; i++)
for(j = i; j < size; j++)
\{
Z = 0;
for(k = i; k $\le$ j; k++)
Z = Z + E[k];
if (Z > Y)
Y = Z;
\}
return Y;
\}
\par\smallskip
The value returned by the function MyX is the \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(A)}~maximum possible sum of elements in any sub-array of array E.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(B)}~maximum element in any sub-array of array E.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(C)}~sum of the maximum elements in all possible sub-arrays of array E.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(D)}~the sum of all the elements in the array E.
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.42} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}

for j = i to n do
for k = j + 1 to n do
D = D * 3 \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(A)}~Half of the product of the 3 consecutive integers.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(B)}~One-third of the product of the 3 consecutive integers.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(C)}~One-sixth of the product of the 3 consecutive integers.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(D)}~None of the above.
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}

\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(A)}~൫(݌$\leftrightarrow$ݍ) ∧ݎ൯∨(݌∧ݍ∧ ∼ݎ)\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(B)}~(∼(݌$\leftrightarrow$ݍ) ∧ݎ) ∨(݌∧ݍ∧∼ݎ)\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent

\noindent\textbf{Q.54} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Given the following schema:
\par\smallskip
employees(emp-id, first-name, last-name, hire-date,
dept-id, salary)
\par\smallskip
departments(dept-id, dept-name, manager-id, location-id)
\par\smallskip
You want to display the last names and hire dates of all latest hires in their respective departments
in the location ID 1700. You issue the following query:
SQL>SELECT last-name, hire-date
FROM employees
WHERE (dept-id, hire-date) IN
(SELECT dept-id, MAX(hire-date)
FROM employees JOIN departments USING(dept-id)
WHERE location-id = 1700
GROUP BY dept-id);
\par\smallskip
What is the outcome? \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(A)}~It executes but does not give the correct result.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(B)}~It executes and gives the correct result.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(C)}~It generates an error because of pairwise comparison.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(D)}~It generates an error because the GROUP BY clause cannot be used with table joins in a sub- \newline query.
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}

of ܲଵ is 1GHz, then the clock frequency of ܲ$^2$ (in GHz) is \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_.
\par\smallskip
CS01 ()

SESSION - 2
1. Total duration of the GATE examination is 180 minutes.
2. The clock will be set at the server. The countdown timer at the top right corner of
screen will display the remaining time available for you to complete the examination.
When the timer reaches zero, the examination will end by itself. You need not
terminate the examination or submit your paper.
3. Any useful data required for your paper can be viewed by clicking on the Useful
Common Data button that appears on the screen.
4. Use the scribble pad provided to you for any rough work. Submit the scribble pad at
the end of the examination.
5. You are allowed to use a non-programmable type calculator, however, sharing of
calculators is not allowed.
6. The Question Palette displayed on the right side of screen will show the status of
each question using one of the following symbols:
The Marked for Review status for a question simply indicates that you would like to look at
that question again. If a question is answered, but marked for review, then the answer will
be considered for evaluation unless the status is modified by the candidate.
Navigating to a Question :
7. To answer a question, do the following:
a. Click on the question number in the Question Palette to go to that question
directly.
b. Select an answer for a multiple choice type question by clicking on the bubble
placed before the 4 choices, namely A, B, C and D. Use the virtual numeric
keypad to enter a number as answer for a numerical type question.
c. Click on Save \& Next to save your answer for the current question and then go
to the next question.
d. Click on Mark for Review \& Next to save your answer for the current question
and also mark it for review, and then go to the next question.
Caution: Note that your answer for the current question will not be saved, if you navigate
to another question directly by clicking on a question number without saving the answer to
the previous question.
You can view all the questions by clicking on the Question Paper button. This feature is
provided, so that if you want you can just see the entire question paper at a glance.
Answering a Question :
8. Procedure for answering a multiple choice (MCQ) type question:
a. Choose one answer from the 4 options (A,B,C,D) given below the question,
click on the bubble placed before the chosen option.
b. To deselect your chosen answer, click on the bubble of the chosen option again
or click on the Clear Response button.
c. To change your chosen answer, click on the bubble of another option.
d. To save your answer, you MUST click on the Save \& Next button.
9. Procedure for answering a numerical answer type question:
a. To enter a number as your answer, use the virtual numerical keypad.
b. A fraction (e.g. -0.3 or -.3) can be entered as an answer with or without '0'
before the decimal point. As many as four decimal points, e.g. 12.5435 or
0.003 or -932.6711 or 12.82 can be entered.
c. To clear your answer, click on the Clear Response button.
d. To save your answer, you MUST click on the Save \& Next button
10. To mark a question for review, click on the Mark for Review \& Next button. If an
answer is selected (for MCQ) or entered (for numerical answer type) for a question
that is Marked for Review, that answer will be considered in the evaluation unless
the status is modified by the candidate.
11. To change your answer to a question that has already been answered, first select
that question for answering and then follow the procedure for answering that type of
question.
12. Note that ONLY Questions for which answers are saved or marked for review after
answering will be considered for evaluation.
Choosing a Section :
13. Sections in this question paper are displayed on the top bar of the screen. Questions
in a Section can be viewed by clicking on the name of that Section. The Section you
are currently viewing will be highlighted.
14. A checkbox is displayed for every optional Section, if any, in the Question Paper. To
select the optional Section for answering, click on the checkbox for that Section.
15. If the checkbox for an optional Section is not selected, the Save \& Next button and
the Mark for Review \& Next button will NOT be enabled for that Section. You will
only be able to see questions in this Section, but you will not be able to answer
questions in the Section.
16. After clicking the Save \& Next button for the last question in a Section, you will
automatically be taken to the first question of the next Section in sequence.
17. You can move the mouse cursor over the name of a Section to view the answering
status for that Section.
Changing the Optional Section :
18. After answering the chosen optional Section, partially or completely, you can change
the optional Section by selecting the checkbox for a new Section that you want to
attempt. A warning message will appear along with a table showing the number of
questions answered in each of the previously chosen optional Sections and a
checkbox against each of these Sections. Click on a checkbox against a Section that
you want to reset and then click on the RESET button. Note that RESETTING a Section
will DELETE all the answers for questions in that Section. Hence, if you think that you
may want to select this Section again later, you will have to note down your answers
for questions in that Section. If you do not want to reset the Section and want to
continue answering the previously chosen optional Section, then click on the BACK
button.
19. If you deselect the checkbox for an optional Section in the top bar, the following
warning message will appear: "Deselecting the checkbox will DELETE all the answers
for questions in this Section. Do you want to deselect this Section?'' If you want to
deselect, click on the RESET button. If you do not want to deselect, click on the BACK
button.
20. You can shuffle between different Sections or change the optional Sections any
number of times.
CS:
Duration: 180 minutes
\par\smallskip
Maximum Marks: 100
Read the following instructions carefully.
1. To login, enter your Registration Number and password provided to you. Kindly go through the various
symbols used in the test and understand their meaning before you start the examination.
2. Once you login and after the start of the examination, you can view all the questions in the question
paper, by clicking on the View All Questions button in the screen.
3. This question paper consists of 2 sections, General Aptitude (GA) for 15 marks and the subject
specific GATE paper for 85 marks. Both these sections are compulsory.
The GA section consists of 10 questions. Question numbers 1 to 5 are of 1-mark each, while question
numbers 6 to 10 are of 2-mark each.
The subject specific GATE paper section consists of 55 questions, out of which question numbers 1 to
25 are of 1-mark each, while question numbers 26 to 55 are of 2-mark each.
4. Depending upon the GATE paper, there may be useful common data that may be required for
answering the questions. If the paper has such useful data, the same can be viewed by clicking on the
Useful Common Data button that appears at the top, right hand side of the screen.
\par\smallskip
5. The computer allotted to you at the examination center runs specialized software that permits only one
answer to be selected for multiple-choice questions using a mouse and to enter a suitable number for
the numerical answer type questions using the virtual keyboard and mouse.
6. Your answers shall be updated and saved on a server periodically and also at the end of the
examination. The examination will stop automatically at the end of 180 minutes.
7. In each paper a candidate can answer a total of 65 questions carrying 100 marks.
8. The question paper may consist of questions of multiple choice type (MCQ) and numerical answer
type.
9. Multiple choice type questions will have four choices against A, B, C, D, out of which only ONE is the
correct answer. The candidate has to choose the correct answer by clicking on the bubble (⃝) placed
before the choice.
10. For numerical answer type questions, each question will have a numerical answer and there will not be
any choices. For these questions, the answer should be enteredby using the virtual keyboard that
appears on the monitor and the mouse.
11. All questions that are not attempted will result in zero marks. However, wrong answers for multiple
choice type questions (MCQ) will result in NEGATIVE marks. For all MCQ questions a wrong
answer will result in deduction of⅓ marks for a 1-mark question and ⅔ marks for a 2-mark question.
12. There is NO NEGATIVE MARKING for questions of NUMERICAL ANSWER TYPE.
13. Non-programmable type Calculator is allowed. Charts, graph sheets, and mathematical tables are NOT
allowed in the Examination Hall. You must use the Scribble pad provided to you at the examination
centre for all your rough work. The Scribble Pad has to be returned at the end of the examination.
\par\smallskip
Declaration by the candidate:
\par\smallskip
``I have read and understood all the above instructions. I have also read and understood clearly the
instructions given on the admit card and shall follow the same. I also understand that in case I am found to
violate any of these instructions, my candidature is liable to be cancelled. I also confirm that at the start of
the examination all the computer hardware allotted to me are in proper working condition''. \par}
\par\vspace{3pt}\noindent\textbf{Answer:} \rule{3.2cm}{0.4pt}
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\end{multicols}

\subsection*{OFFICIAL ANSWER KEY --- GATE 2014 (CS)}
\vspace{-4pt}
\noindent{\footnotesize\textit{Official final answer keys published by the GATE Organising Institute. For NAT questions, allowable numerical ranges are displayed where applicable. MTA = Marks To All.}}

\noindent\textbf{Q.1} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Out of the following four sentences, select the most suitable sentence with respect to grammar and
usage. \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(A)}~I will not leave the place until the minister does not meet me.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(B)}~I will not leave the place until the minister doesn't meet me.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(C)}~I will not leave the place until the minister meet me.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(D)}~I will not leave the place until the minister meets me.
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}

\noindent\textbf{Q.7} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Indian currency notes show the denomination indicated in at least seventeen languages. If this is not
an indication of the nation's diversity, nothing else is.
\par\smallskip
Which of the following can be logically inferred from the above sentences? \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(A)}~India is a country of exactly seventeen languages.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(B)}~Linguistic pluralism is the only indicator of a nation's diversity.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(C)}~Indian currency notes have sufficient space for all the Indian languages.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(D)}~Linguistic pluralism is strong evidence of India's diversity.
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}

II.
R always beats S
III.

IV.

\par\smallskip

(i)
P is likely to beat all the three other players
(ii)
S is the absolute worst player in the set \par}

\textbf{(C)}~(i) and (ii) & \textbf{(D)}~neither (i) nor (ii)
\end{tabular*}
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}

. \par}

implement the computation F(G(Xi)) for 1 $\le$i $\le$10. Ignoring all other delays, the minimum
time required to complete this computation is
nanoseconds. \par}
\par\vspace{3pt}\noindent\textbf{Answer:} \rule{3.2cm}{0.4pt}
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.31} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[NAT]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries The size of the data count register of a DMA controller is 16 bits. The processor needs to
transfer a ﬁle of 29,154 kilobytes from disk to main memory. The memory is byte addressable.
The minimum number of times the DMA controller needs to get the control of the system bus
from the processor to transfer the ﬁle from the disk to main memory is
. \par}
\par\vspace{3pt}\noindent\textbf{Answer:} \rule{3.2cm}{0.4pt}
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.32} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[NAT]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries The stage delays in a 4-stage pipeline are 800, 500, 400 and 300 picoseconds. The ﬁrst
stage (with delay 800 picoseconds) is replaced with a functionally equivalent design involving
two stages with respective delays 600 and 350 picoseconds. The throughput increase of the
pipeline is
percent. \par}
\par\vspace{3pt}\noindent\textbf{Answer:} \rule{3.2cm}{0.4pt}
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.33} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Consider a carry lookahead adder for adding two n-bit integers, built using gates of fan-in at
most two. The time to perform addition using this adder is \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(A)}~Θ(1)\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(B)}~Θ(log(n))\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(C)}~Θ($\sqrt{\vphantom{x}}$n)\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(D)}~Θ(n) \newline CS(Set A)
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.34} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries The following function computes the maximum value contained in an integer array p[] of size
n (n $\ge$ 1).
int max(int *p, int n) \{
int a=0, b=n-1;
while (\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_) \{
if (p[a] $\le$ p[b]) \{ a = a+1; \}
else
\{ b = b-1; \}
\}
return p[a];
\}
The missing loop condition is \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular*}{\columnwidth}{@{\extracolsep{\fill}}p{0.46\columnwidth}p{0.46\columnwidth}@{}}
\textbf{(A)}~a $\ne$ n & \textbf{(B)}~b $\ne$ 0 \\
\addlinespace[2pt]
\textbf{(C)}~b > (a + 1) & \textbf{(D)}~b $\ne$ a
\end{tabular*}
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.35} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries What will be the output of the following C program?
void count(int n)\{
static int d=1;
printf("\%d ", n);
printf("\%d ", d);
d++;
if(n>1) count(n-1);
printf("\%d ", d);
\}
void main()\{
count(3);
\} \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(A)}~3 1 2 2 1 3 4 4 4\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(B)}~3 1 2 1 1 1 2 2 2\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(C)}~3 1 2 2 1 3 4\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(D)}~3 1 2 1 1 1 2 \newline CS(Set A)
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.36} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries What will be the output of the following pseudo-code when parameters are passed by reference
and dynamic scoping is assumed?
a=3;
void n(x) \{x = x * a; print(x);\}
void m(y) \{a = 1; a = y - a; n(a); print(a);\}
void main() \{m(a);\} \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular*}{\columnwidth}{@{\extracolsep{\fill}}p{0.46\columnwidth}p{0.46\columnwidth}@{}}
\textbf{(A)}~6, 2 & \textbf{(B)}~6, 6 \\
\addlinespace[2pt]
\textbf{(C)}~4, 2 & \textbf{(D)}~4, 4
\end{tabular*}
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.37} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries An operator delete(i) for a binary heap data structure is to be designed to delete the item in
the i-th node. Assume that the heap is implemented in an array and i refers to the i-th index
of the array. If the heap tree has depth d (number of edges on the path from the root to the
farthest leaf), then what is the time complexity to re-ﬁx the heap efﬁciently after the removal
of the element? \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular*}{\columnwidth}{@{\extracolsep{\fill}}p{0.46\columnwidth}p{0.46\columnwidth}@{}}
\textbf{(A)}~O(1) & \textbf{(B)}~O(d) but not O(1) \\
\addlinespace[2pt]
\textbf{(C)}~O(2d) but not O(d) & \textbf{(D)}~O(d 2d) but not O(2d)
\end{tabular*}
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}

\par\vspace{2pt}

of vertices will contain the edge with weight x is
. \par}

\noindent\textbf{Q.39} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[NAT]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Let G be a complete undirected graph on 4 vertices, having 6 edges with weights being 1, 2,
3, 4, 5, and 6. The maximum possible weight that a minimum weight spanning tree of G can
have is
.
CS(Set A) \par}
\par\vspace{3pt}\noindent\textbf{Answer:} \rule{3.2cm}{0.4pt}
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.40} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries G = (V,E) is an undirected simple graph in which each edge has a distinct weight, and e is a
particular edge of G. Which of the following statements about the minimum spanning trees
(MSTs) of G is/are TRUE?
I. If e is the lightest edge of some cycle in G, then every MST of G includes e
II. If e is the heaviest edge of some cycle in G, then every MST of G excludes e \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular*}{\columnwidth}{@{\extracolsep{\fill}}p{0.46\columnwidth}p{0.46\columnwidth}@{}}
\textbf{(A)}~I only & \textbf{(B)}~II only \\
\addlinespace[2pt]
\textbf{(C)}~both I and II & \textbf{(D)}~neither I nor II
\end{tabular*}
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.41} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[NAT]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Let Q denote a queue containing sixteen numbers and S be an empty stack. Head(Q) returns
the element at the head of the queue Q without removing it from Q. Similarly Top(S) returns
the element at the top of S without removing it from S. Consider the algorithm given below.
while Q is not Empty do
if S is Empty OR Top(S) $\le$Head(Q) then
x := Dequeue(Q);
Push(S,x);
else
x := Pop(S);
Enqueue(Q,x);
end
end
The maximum possible number of iterations of the while loop in the algorithm is
. \par}
\par\vspace{3pt}\noindent\textbf{Answer:} \rule{3.2cm}{0.4pt}
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.42} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Consider the following context-free grammars:
G1: S $\to$aS|B, B $\to$b|bB
G2: S $\to$aA|bB, A $\to$aA|B|$\epsilon$, B $\to$bB|$\epsilon$
Which one of the following pairs of languages is generated by G1 and G2, respectively? \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(A)}~\{ambn|m > 0 or n > 0\} and \{ambn|m > 0 and n > 0\}\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(B)}~\{ambn|m > 0 and n > 0\} and \{ambn|m > 0 or n $\ge$0\}\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(C)}~\{ambn|m $\ge$0 or n > 0\} and \{ambn|m > 0 and n > 0\}\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(D)}~\{ambn|m $\ge$0 and n > 0\} and \{ambn|m > 0 or n > 0\} \newline CS(Set A)
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.43} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Consider the following languages:
L1 = \{anbmcn+m : m,n $\ge$1\}
L2 = \{anbnc2n : n $\ge$1\}
Which one of the following is TRUE? \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(A)}~Both L1 and L2 are context-free.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(B)}~L1 is context-free while L2 is not context-free.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(C)}~L2 is context-free while L1 is not context-free.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(D)}~Neither L1 nor L2 is context-free. \newline CS(Set B)
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.44} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Let X be a recursive language and Y be a recursively enumerable but not recursive language.
Let W and Z be two languages such that Y reduces to W, and Z reduces to X (reduction means
the standard many-one reduction). Which one of the following statements is TRUE? \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(A)}~W can be recursively enumerable and Z is recursive.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(B)}~W can be recursive and Z is recursively enumerable.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(C)}~W is not recursively enumerable and Z is recursive.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(D)}~W is not recursively enumerable and Z is not recursive.
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}

replacement policy and the optimal page replacement policy is

\noindent\textbf{Q.50} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Consider the following proposed solution for the critical section problem.
There are n
processes: P0 ...Pn−1. In the code, function pmax returns an integer not smaller than any
of its arguments. For all i, t[i] is initialized to zero.
Code for Pi:
do \{
c[i]=1; t[i] = pmax(t[0],...,t[n-1])+1; c[i]=0;
for every j̸ = i in \{0,...,n-1\} \{
while (c[j]);
while (t[j] $\ne$ 0 \&\& t[j]$\le$t[i]);
\}
Critical Section;
t[i]=0;
Remainder Section;
\} while (true);
Which one of the following is TRUE about the above solution? \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(A)}~At most one process can be in the critical section at any time\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(B)}~The bounded wait condition is satisﬁed\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(C)}~The progress condition is satisﬁed\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(D)}~It cannot cause a deadlock
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.51} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Consider the following two phase locking protocol. Suppose a transaction T accesses (for
read or write operations), a certain set of objects \{O1,...,Ok\}. This is done in the following
manner:
Step 1. T acquires exclusive locks to O1, ..., Ok in increasing order of their addresses.
Step 2. The required operations are performed.
Step 3. All locks are released.
This protocol will \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(A)}~guarantee serializability and deadlock-freedom\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(B)}~guarantee neither serializability nor deadlock-freedom\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(C)}~guarantee serializability but not deadlock-freedom\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(D)}~guarantee deadlock-freedom but not serializability \newline CS(Set A)
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.52} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Consider that B wants to send a message m that is digitally signed to A. Let the pair of private
and public keys for A and B be denoted by K−
x and K+
x for x = A,B, respectively. Let Kx(m)
represent the operation of encrypting m with a key Kx and H(m) represent the message digest.
Which one of the following indicates the CORRECT way of sending the message m along
with the digital signature to A? \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(A)}~\{m,K+ \newline B (H(m))\}\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(B)}~\{m,K− \newline B (H(m))\}\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(C)}~\{m,K− \newline A (H(m))\}\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(D)}~\{m,K+ \newline A (m)\}
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.53} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[NAT]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries An IP datagram of size 1000 bytes arrives at a router. The router has to forward this packet on
a link whose MTU (maximum transmission unit) is 100 bytes. Assume that the size of the IP
header is 20 bytes.
The number of fragments that the IP datagram will be divided into for transmission is
. \par}

seconds. \par}

\noindent\textbf{Q.1} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries ``From where are they bringing their books? \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_ bringing \_\_\_\_\_\_\_ books from
\_\_\_\_\_.''
\par\smallskip
The words that best fill the blanks in the above sentence are \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular*}{\columnwidth}{@{\extracolsep{\fill}}p{0.46\columnwidth}p{0.46\columnwidth}@{}}
\textbf{(A)}~Their, they're, there & \textbf{(B)}~They're, their, there \\
\addlinespace[2pt]
\textbf{(C)}~There, their, they're & \textbf{(D)}~They're, there, there
\end{tabular*}
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.1} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Which one of the following is a closed form expression for the generating function of the
sequence \{an\}, where an = 2n + 3 for all n = 0, 1, 2,… ? \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(A)}~2 \newline 3 \newline (1 \newline )x \newline −\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(B)}~2 \newline 3 \newline (1 \newline ) \newline x \newline x \newline −\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(C)}~2 \newline 2 \newline (1 \newline ) \newline x \newline x \newline − \newline −\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(D)}~2 \newline 3 \newline (1 \newline ) \newline x \newline x \newline − \newline −
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.2} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries ``A \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_ investigation can sometimes yield new facts, but typically organized ones
are more successful.''
\par\smallskip
The word that best fills the blank in the above sentence is \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular*}{\columnwidth}{@{\extracolsep{\fill}}p{0.46\columnwidth}p{0.46\columnwidth}@{}}
\textbf{(A)}~meandering & \textbf{(B)}~timely \\
\addlinespace[2pt]
\textbf{(C)}~consistent & \textbf{(D)}~systematic
\end{tabular*}
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.2} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Consider the following C program.
\par\smallskip
\#include<stdio.h>
struct Ournode\{
char x,y,z;
\};
\par\smallskip
int main()\{
struct Ournode p = \{'1', '0', 'a'+2\};
struct Ournode *q = \&p;
printf ("\%c, \%c", *((char*)q+1), *((char*)q+2));
return 0;
\}
\par\smallskip
The output of this program is: \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular*}{\columnwidth}{@{\extracolsep{\fill}}p{0.46\columnwidth}p{0.46\columnwidth}@{}}
\textbf{(A)}~0, c & \textbf{(B)}~0, a+2 \\
\addlinespace[2pt]
\textbf{(C)}~'0', 'a+2' & \textbf{(D)}~'0', 'c'
\end{tabular*}
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}

\textbf{(C)}~𝑃̅ $\oplus$𝑄̅ = 𝑃$\oplus$𝑄\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent

\noindent\textbf{Q.6} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}

equivalent to N. Which one of the following is necessarily true? \par}

\textbf{(A)}~$k$$\ge$2$n$ & \textbf{(B)}~$k$$\ge$$n$ \\
\addlinespace[2pt]
\textbf{(C)}~$k$$\le$$n$2 & \textbf{(D)}~$k$$\le$2$n$
\end{tabular*}
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}

money (in Rupees) did the philanthropist give away in total? \par}

\addlinespace[2pt]
\textbf{(C)}~1,75,000 & \textbf{(D)}~1,51,000
\end{tabular*}

\noindent\textbf{Q.8} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries In a party, 60\% of the invited guests are male and 40\% are female. If 80\% of the invited
guests attended the party and if all the invited female guests attended, what would be the
ratio of males to females among the attendees in the party? \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular*}{\columnwidth}{@{\extracolsep{\fill}}p{0.46\columnwidth}p{0.46\columnwidth}@{}}

\end{tabular*}

\noindent\textbf{Q.9} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries The following are some events that occur after a device controller issues an interrupt while
process L is under execution.
(P) The processor pushes the process status of L onto the control stack.
(Q) The processor finishes the execution of the current instruction.
(R) The processor executes the interrupt service routine.
(S) The processor pops the process status of L from the control stack.
(T) The processor loads the new PC value based on the interrupt.
Which one of the following is the correct order in which the events above occur? \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular*}{\columnwidth}{@{\extracolsep{\fill}}p{0.46\columnwidth}p{0.46\columnwidth}@{}}
\textbf{(A)}~QPTRS & \textbf{(B)}~PTRSQ \\
\addlinespace[2pt]
\textbf{(C)}~TRPQS & \textbf{(D)}~QTPRS
\end{tabular*}
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.10} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Consider a process executing on an operating system that uses demand paging. The average
time for a memory access in the system is M units if the corresponding memory page is
available in memory, and D units if the memory access causes a page fault. It has been
experimentally measured that the average time taken for a memory access in the process is
X units.
\par\smallskip
Which one of the following is the correct expression for the page fault rate experienced by
the process? \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular*}{\columnwidth}{@{\extracolsep{\fill}}p{0.46\columnwidth}p{0.46\columnwidth}@{}}
\textbf{(A)}~(D -- M) / (X -- M) & \textbf{(B)}~(X -- M) / (D -- M) \\
\addlinespace[2pt]
\textbf{(C)}~(D -- X) / (D -- M) & \textbf{(D)}~(X -- M) / (D -- X)
\end{tabular*}
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}

\vspace{6pt}
\noindent{\footnotesize\textit{[Section Block: 2-mark questions]}}
\vspace{3pt}

\noindent\textbf{Q.10} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries A six sided unbiased die with four green faces and two red faces is rolled seven times.
Which of the following combinations is the most likely outcome of the experiment? \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(A)}~Three green faces and four red faces.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(B)}~Four green faces and three red faces.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(C)}~Five green faces and two red faces.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(D)}~Six green faces and one red face.
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}

\vspace{6pt}
\noindent{\footnotesize\textit{[Section Block: 1-mark questions]}}

\noindent\textbf{Q.11} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries In an Entity-Relationship (ER) model, suppose 𝑅 is a many-to-one relationship from entity
set E1 to entity set E2. Assume that E1 and E2 participate totally in 𝑅 and that the cardinality
of E1 is greater than the cardinality of E2.
\par\smallskip
Which one of the following is true about 𝑅? \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(A)}~Every entity in E1 is associated with exactly one entity in E2.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(B)}~Some entity in E1 is associated with more than one entity in E2.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(C)}~Every entity in E2 is associated with exactly one entity in E1.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(D)}~Every entity in E2 is associated with at most one entity in E1.
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.12} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Consider the following two tables and four queries in SQL.
Book (isbn, bname), Stock (isbn, copies)
\par\smallskip
Query 1: SELECT B.isbn, S.copies
FROM Book B INNER JOIN Stock S
ON B.isbn = S.isbn;
\par\smallskip
Query 2: SELECT B.isbn, S.copies
FROM Book B LEFT OUTER JOIN Stock S
ON B.isbn = S.isbn;
\par\smallskip
Query 3: SELECT B.isbn, S.copies
FROM Book B RIGHT OUTER JOIN Stock S
ON B.isbn = S.isbn;
\par\smallskip
Query 4: SELECT B.isbn, S.copies
FROM Book B FULL OUTER JOIN Stock S
ON B.isbn = S.isbn;
\par\smallskip
Which one of the queries above is certain to have an output that is a superset of the outputs
of the other three queries? \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular*}{\columnwidth}{@{\extracolsep{\fill}}p{0.46\columnwidth}p{0.46\columnwidth}@{}}
\textbf{(A)}~Query 1 & \textbf{(B)}~Query 2 \\
\addlinespace[2pt]
\textbf{(C)}~Query 3 & \textbf{(D)}~Query 4
\end{tabular*}
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}

{\bfseries Match the following:
Field
Length in bits
P. UDP Header's Port Number
I. 48
Q. Ethernet MAC Address
II. 8
R. IPv6 Next Header
III. 32

\noindent\textbf{Q.14} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Consider the following statements regarding the slow start phase of the TCP congestion
control algorithm. Note that cwnd stands for the TCP congestion window and MSS denotes
the Maximum Segment Size.
(i) The cwnd increases by 2 MSS on every successful acknowledgment.
(ii) The cwnd approximately doubles on every successful acknowledgement.
(iii) The cwnd increases by 1 MSS every round trip time.
(iv) The cwnd approximately doubles every round trip time.
\par\smallskip
Which one of the following is correct? \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(A)}~Only (ii) and (iii) are true\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(B)}~Only (i) and (iii) are true\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(C)}~Only (iv) is true\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(D)}~Only (i) and (iv) are true
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}

all 6 numbers on each dice are equi-probable and that all trials are independent. The
probability (rounded to 3 decimal places) that one of them wins on the third trial is \_\_\_\_\_. \par}

\noindent\textbf{Q.21} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[NAT]}}

{\bfseries Consider the following C program:
\#include <stdio.h>
\par\smallskip
int counter = 0;
\par\smallskip
int calc (int a, int b) \{
int c;
\par\smallskip
counter++;
if (b==3) return (a*a*a);
else \{
c = calc(a, b/3);
return (c*c*c);
\}
\}
\par\smallskip
int main ()\{
calc(4, 81);
printf ("\%d", counter);
\}
\par\smallskip
The output of this program is \_\_\_\_\_. \par}
\par\vspace{3pt}\noindent\textbf{Answer:} \rule{3.2cm}{0.4pt}
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}

\noindent\textbf{Q.28} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}

{\bfseries Consider the first-order logic sentence
𝜑$\equiv$ ∃𝑠∃𝑡∃𝑢∀𝑣∀𝑤∀$x$∀$y$ 𝜓(𝑠, 𝑡, 𝑢, 𝑣, 𝑤, $x$, $y$)
\par\smallskip
where 𝜓(𝑠, 𝑡, 𝑢, 𝑣, 𝑤, $x$, $y$) is a quantifier-free first-order logic formula using only predicate
symbols, and possibly equality, but no function symbols. Suppose 𝜑 has a model with a
universe containing 7 elements.
\par\smallskip
Which one of the following statements is necessarily true? \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(A)}~There exists at least one model of 𝜑 with universe of size less than or equal to 3.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(B)}~There exists no model of 𝜑 with universe of size less than or equal to 3.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(C)}~There exists no model of 𝜑 with universe of size greater than 7.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(D)}~Every model of 𝜑 has a universe of size equal to 7.

\noindent\textbf{Q.29} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Consider the following C program:
\#include<stdio.h>
\par\smallskip
void fun1(char *s1, char *s2)\{
\par\smallskip
char *tmp;
\par\smallskip
tmp = s1;
\par\smallskip
s1 = s2;
\par\smallskip
s2 = tmp;
\}
void fun2(char **s1, char **s2)\{
\par\smallskip
char *tmp;
\par\smallskip
tmp = *s1;
\par\smallskip
*s1 = *s2;
\par\smallskip
*s2 = tmp;
\}
int main()\{
\par\smallskip
char *str1 = "Hi", *str2 = "Bye";
\par\smallskip
fun1(str1, str2); printf("\%s \%s ", str1, str2);
\par\smallskip
fun2(\&str1, \&str2); printf("\%s \%s", str1, str2);
\par\smallskip
return 0;
\}
\par\smallskip
The output of the program above is \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular*}{\columnwidth}{@{\extracolsep{\fill}}p{0.46\columnwidth}p{0.46\columnwidth}@{}}
\textbf{(A)}~Hi Bye Bye Hi & \textbf{(B)}~Hi Bye Hi Bye \\
\addlinespace[2pt]
\textbf{(C)}~Bye Hi Hi Bye & \textbf{(D)}~Bye Hi Bye Hi
\end{tabular*}

\noindent\textbf{Q.40} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Consider the following solution to the producer-consumer synchronization problem. The
shared buffer size is 𝑁. Three semaphores empty, full and mutex are defined with respective
initial values of 0, 𝑁 and 1. Semaphore empty denotes the number of available slots in the
buffer, for the consumer to read from. Semaphore full denotes the number of available slots
in the buffer, for the producer to write to. The placeholder variables, denoted by P, Q, R,
and S, in the code below can be assigned either empty or full. The valid semaphore
operations are: wait() and signal().
Producer:
Consumer:
\par\smallskip
do\{
wait(P);
wait(mutex);
//Add item to buffer
signal(mutex);
signal(Q);
\}while(1);
do\{
wait(R);
wait(mutex);
//Consume item from buffer
signal(mutex);
signal(S);
\}while(1);
\par\smallskip
Which one of the following assignments to P, Q, R and S will yield the correct solution? \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(A)}~P: full, Q: full, R: empty, S: empty\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(B)}~P: empty, Q: empty, R: full, S: full\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(C)}~P: full, Q: empty, R: empty, S: full\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(D)}~P: empty, Q: full, R: full, S: empty

\par\vspace{3pt}\noindent\textbf{Answer:} \rule{3.2cm}{0.4pt}
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.44} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[NAT]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Consider Guwahati (G) and Delhi (D) whose temperatures can be classified as high (𝐻),
medium (𝑀) and low (𝐿). Let 𝑃(𝐻𝐺) denote the probability that Guwahati has high
temperature. Similarly, 𝑃(𝑀𝐺) and 𝑃(𝐿𝐺) denotes the probability of Guwahati having
medium and low temperatures respectively. Similarly, we use 𝑃(𝐻𝐷), 𝑃(𝑀𝐷) and 𝑃(𝐿𝐷) for
Delhi.
\par\smallskip
The following table gives the conditional probabilities for Delhi's temperature given
Guwahati's temperature.
𝐻𝐷
𝑀𝐷
𝐿𝐷
𝐻𝐺
0.40
0.48
0.12
𝑀𝐺
0.10
0.65
0.25
𝐿𝐺
0.01
0.50
0.49
\par\smallskip
Consider the first row in the table above. The first entry denotes that if Guwahati has high
temperature (𝐻𝐺) then the probability of Delhi also having a high temperature (𝐻𝐷) is 0.40;
i.e., 𝑃(𝐻𝐷|𝐻𝐺) = 0.40. Similarly, the next two entries are 𝑃(𝑀𝐷|𝐻𝐺) = 0.48 and
𝑃(𝐿𝐷|𝐻𝐺) = 0.12. Similarly for the other rows.
\par\smallskip
If it is known that 𝑃(𝐻𝐺) = 0.2, 𝑃(𝑀𝐺) = 0.5, and 𝑃(𝐿𝐺) = 0.3, then the probability
(correct to two decimal places) that Guwahati has high temperature given that Delhi has high
temperature is \_\_\_\_\_\_\_. \par}
\par\vspace{3pt}\noindent\textbf{Answer:} \rule{3.2cm}{0.4pt}
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.45} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[NAT]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Consider the following program written in pseudo-code. Assume that x and y are integers.
Count(x,y) \{
if (y $\ne$ 1)\{
if (x $\ne$ 1) \{
print("*");
Count(x/2, y);
\}
else \{
y = y-1;
Count(1024, y);
\}
\}
\}
\par\smallskip
The number of times that the print statement is executed by the call
Count(1024,1024) is \_\_\_\_\_. \par}
\par\vspace{3pt}\noindent\textbf{Answer:} \rule{3.2cm}{0.4pt}
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.46} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[NAT]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries The number of possible min-heaps containing each value from \{1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7\}exactly once
is \_\_\_\_\_. \par}
\par\vspace{3pt}\noindent\textbf{Answer:} \rule{3.2cm}{0.4pt}
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.48} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[NAT]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Consider the weights and values of items listed below. Note that there is only one unit of
each item.
Item number
Weight
(in Kgs)
Value
(in Rupees)
1
10
60
2
7
28
3
4
20
4
2
24
\par\smallskip
The task is to pick a subset of these items such that their total weight is no more than 11 Kgs
and their total value is maximized. Moreover, no item may be split. The total value of items
picked by an optimal algorithm is denoted by 𝑉opt. A greedy algorithm sorts the items by
their value-to-weight ratios in descending order and packs them greedily, starting from the
first item in the ordered list. The total value of items picked by the greedy algorithm is
denoted by 𝑉greedy.
\par\smallskip
The value of 𝑉opt −𝑉greedy is \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_. \par}
\par\vspace{3pt}\noindent\textbf{Answer:} \rule{3.2cm}{0.4pt}
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.50} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[NAT]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries The instruction pipeline of a RISC processor has the following stages: Instruction Fetch (IF),
Instruction Decode (ID), Operand Fetch (OF), Perform Operation (PO) and Writeback (WB).
The IF, ID, OF and WB stages take 1 clock cycle each for every instruction. Consider a
sequence of 100 instructions. In the PO stage, 40 instructions take 3 clock cycles each, 35
instructions take 2 clock cycles each, and the remaining 25 instructions take 1 clock cycle
each. Assume that there are no data hazards and no control hazards.
\par\smallskip
The number of clock cycles required for completion of execution of the sequence of
instructions is \_\_\_\_\_\_. \par}
\par\vspace{3pt}\noindent\textbf{Answer:} \rule{3.2cm}{0.4pt}
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.51} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[NAT]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries A processor has 16 integer registers (R0, R1, .. , R15) and 64 floating point registers (F0,
F1,… , F63). It uses a 2-byte instruction format. There are four categories of instructions:
Type-1, Type-2, Type-3, and Type-4. Type-1 category consists of four instructions, each
with 3 integer register operands (3Rs). Type-2 category consists of eight instructions, each
with 2 floating point register operands (2Fs). Type-3 category consists of fourteen
instructions, each with one integer register operand and one floating point register operand
(1R+1F). Type-4 category consists of N instructions, each with a floating point register
operand (1F).
\par\smallskip
The maximum value of N is \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_. \par}
\par\vspace{3pt}\noindent\textbf{Answer:} \rule{3.2cm}{0.4pt}
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.52} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[NAT]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Given a language 𝐿, define 𝐿$i$ as follows:
𝐿0 = \{𝜀\}
𝐿$i$= 𝐿$i$−1 ⋅𝐿 𝑓𝑜$r$ 𝑎𝑙𝑙 $i$> 0
\par\smallskip
The order of a language L is defined as the smallest k such that 𝐿$k$= 𝐿$k$+1.
Consider the language L1 (over alphabet 0) accepted by the following automaton.
\par\smallskip
The order of L1 is \_\_\_\_\_.
0
0
0 \par}
\par\vspace{3pt}\noindent\textbf{Answer:} \rule{3.2cm}{0.4pt}
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.53} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[NAT]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Consider a storage disk with 4 platters (numbered as 0, 1, 2 and 3), 200 cylinders (numbered
as 0, 1, … , 199), and 256 sectors per track (numbered as 0, 1, … , 255). The following 6
disk requests of the form [sector number, cylinder number, platter number] are received by
the disk controller at the same time:
[120, 72, 2] , [180, 134, 1] , [60, 20, 0] , [212, 86, 3] , [56, 116, 2] , [118, 16, 1]
\par\smallskip
Currently the head is positioned at sector number 100 of cylinder 80, and is moving towards
higher cylinder numbers. The average power dissipation in moving the head over 100
cylinders is 20 milliwatts and for reversing the direction of the head movement once is 15
milliwatts. Power dissipation associated with rotational latency and switching of head
between different platters is negligible.
\par\smallskip
The total power consumption in milliwatts to satisfy all of the above disk requests using the
Shortest Seek Time First disk scheduling algorithm is \_\_\_\_\_\_\_. \par}
\par\vspace{3pt}\noindent\textbf{Answer:} \rule{3.2cm}{0.4pt}
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.54} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[NAT]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Consider an IP packet with a length of 4,500 bytes that includes a 20-byte IPv4 header and
a 40-byte TCP header. The packet is forwarded to an IPv4 router that supports a Maximum
Transmission Unit (MTU) of 600 bytes. Assume that the length of the IP header in all the
outgoing fragments of this packet is 20 bytes. Assume that the fragmentation offset value
stored in the first fragment is 0.
\par\smallskip
The fragmentation offset value stored in the third fragment is \_\_\_\_\_\_\_. \par}
\par\vspace{3pt}\noindent\textbf{Answer:} \rule{3.2cm}{0.4pt}
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\end{multicols}
