\noindent\textbf{Q.41} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Let $A$ be a $2 \times 2$ matrix given by:
\[ A = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 1 \\ 1 & -1 \end{bmatrix} \]
What are the eigenvalues of the matrix $A^{13}$? \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(A)}~$1, -1$\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(B)}~$2\sqrt{2}, -2\sqrt{2}$\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(C)}~$4\sqrt{2}, -4\sqrt{2}$\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(D)}~$64\sqrt{2}, -64\sqrt{2}$
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.42} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Consider the following four variable Boolean function in sum-of-product form:
\[ F(b\_3, b\_2, b\_1, b\_0) = \sum(0, 2, 4, 8, 10, 11, 12) \]
where the value of the function is computed by considering $b_3b_2b_1b_0$ as a 4-bit binary number, where $b_3$ denotes the most significant bit and $b_0$ denotes the least significant bit. Note that there are no don't care terms. Which ONE of the following options is the CORRECT minimized expression? \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(A)}~$\overline{b}_1\overline{b}_0 + \overline{b}_2\overline{b}_0 + b_1\overline{b}_2b_3$\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(B)}~$\overline{b}_1\overline{b}_0 + \overline{b}_2\overline{b}_0$\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(C)}~$\overline{b}_2\overline{b}_0 + b_1b_2b_3$\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(D)}~$\overline{b}_0\overline{b}_2 + \overline{b}_3$
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.43} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Let $G(V, E)$ be an undirected and unweighted graph with 100 vertices. Let $d(u, v)$ denote the number of edges in a shortest path between vertices $u$ and $v$ in $V$. Let the maximum value of $d(u, v), u, v \in V$ such that $u \neq v$, be 30. Let $T$ be any breadth-first-search tree of $G$. Which ONE of the given options is CORRECT for every such graph $G$? \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(A)}~The height of $T$ is exactly 15.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(B)}~The height of $T$ is exactly 30.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(C)}~The height of $T$ is at least 15.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(D)}~The height of $T$ is at least 30.
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.44} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Consider the following two languages over the alphabet $\{a, b\}$:
\[ L\_1 = \{ \alpha\beta\alpha \mid \alpha \in \{a, b\}^+ \text{ AND } \beta \in \{a, b\}^+ \} \]
\[ L\_2 = \{ \alpha\beta\alpha \mid \alpha \in \{a\}^+ \text{ AND } \beta \in \{a, b\}^+ \} \]
Which ONE of the following statements is CORRECT? \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(A)}~Both $L_1$ and $L_2$ are regular languages.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(B)}~$L_1$ is a regular language but $L_2$ is not a regular language.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(C)}~$L_1$ is not a regular language but $L_2$ is a regular language.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(D)}~Neither $L_1$ nor $L_2$ is a regular language.
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.45} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Consider the following two languages over the alphabet $\{a, b, c\}$, where $m$ and $n$ are natural numbers.
\[ L\_1 = \{a^mb^mc^{m+n} \mid m, n \ge 1\} \]
\[ L\_2 = \{a^mb^nc^{m+n} \mid m, n \ge 1\} \]
Which ONE of the following statements is CORRECT? \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(A)}~Both $L_1$ and $L_2$ are context-free languages.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(B)}~$L_1$ is a context-free language but $L_2$ is not a context-free language.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(C)}~$L_1$ is not a context-free language but $L_2$ is a context-free language.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(D)}~Neither $L_1$ nor $L_2$ are context-free languages.
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.46} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MSQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Which of the following statement(s) is/are TRUE while computing First and Follow during top down parsing by a compiler? \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(A)}~For a production $A \to \epsilon$, $\epsilon$ will be added to $First(A)$.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(B)}~If there is any input right end marker, it will be added to $First(S)$, where $S$ is the start symbol.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(C)}~For a production $A \to \epsilon$, $\epsilon$ will be added to $Follow(A)$.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(D)}~If there is any input right end marker, it will be added to $Follow(S)$, where $S$ is the start symbol.
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.47} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MSQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Consider a relational schema $team(name, city, owner)$, with functional dependencies $\{name \to city, name \to owner\}$. The relation $team$ is decomposed into two relations, $t_1(name, city)$ and $t_2(name, owner)$. Which of the following statement(s) is/are TRUE? \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(A)}~The relation $team$ is NOT in BCNF.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(B)}~The relations $t_1$ and $t_2$ are in BCNF.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(C)}~The decomposition constitutes a lossless join.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(D)}~The relation $team$ is NOT in 3NF.
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.48} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MSQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Which of the following predicate logic formulae/formula is/are CORRECT representation(s) of the statement: ``Everyone has exactly one mother''?
The meanings of the predicates used are:
\begin{itemize}
\item $mother(y, x)$: $y$ is the mother of $x$
\item $noteq(x, y)$: $x$ and $y$ are not equal
\end{itemize} \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(A)}~$\forall x \exists y \exists z (mother(y, x) \land \neg mother(z, x))$\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(B)}~$\forall x \exists y [mother(y, x) \land \forall z (noteq(z, y) \to \neg mother(z, x))]$\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(C)}~$\forall x \forall y [mother(y, x) \to \exists z (mother(z, x) \land \neg noteq(z, y))]$\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(D)}~$\forall x \exists y [mother(y, x) \land \neg \exists z (noteq(z, y) \land mother(z, x))]$
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.49} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MSQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries $A = \{0, 1, 2, 3, \dots \}$ is the set of non-negative integers. Let $\mathcal{F}$ be the set of functions from $A$ to itself. For any two functions $f_1, f_2 \in \mathcal{F}$, we define
\[ (f\_1 \odot f\_2)(n) = f\_1(n) + f\_2(n) \]
for every number $n$ in $A$. Which of the following is/are CORRECT about the mathematical structure $(\mathcal{F}, \odot)$? \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(A)}~$(\mathcal{F}, \odot)$ is an Abelian group.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(B)}~$(\mathcal{F}, \odot)$ is an Abelian monoid.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(C)}~$(\mathcal{F}, \odot)$ is a non-Abelian group.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(D)}~$(\mathcal{F}, \odot)$ is a non-Abelian monoid.
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.50} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MSQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Consider the deterministic finite automaton (DFA) defined over the alphabet $\Sigma = \{a, b\}$. Identify which of the following language(s) is/are accepted by the given DFA. \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(A)}~The set of all strings containing an even number of $b$'s.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(B)}~The set of all strings containing the pattern $bab$.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(C)}~The set of all strings ending with the pattern $bab$.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(D)}~The set of all strings not containing the pattern $aba$.
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.51} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[NAT]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries A disk of size 512M bytes is divided into blocks of 64K bytes. A file is stored in the disk using linked allocation. In linked allocation, each data block reserves 4 bytes to store the pointer to the next data block. The link part of the last data block contains a NULL pointer (also of 4 bytes). Suppose a file of 1M bytes needs to be stored in the disk. Assume $1\text{K} = 2^{10}$ and $1\text{M} = 2^{20}$. The amount of space in bytes that will be wasted due to internal fragmentation is \_\_\_\_\_\_\. (Answer in integer) \par}
\par\vspace{3pt}\noindent\textbf{Answer:} \rule{3.2cm}{0.4pt}
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.52} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[NAT]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Refer to the given 3-address code sequence. This code sequence is split into basic blocks. The number of basic blocks is \_\_\_\_\_\_\. (Answer in integer)
\par\smallskip
\begin{verbatim}
1001: i = 1
1002: j = 1
1003: t1 = 10*i
1004: t2 = t1+j
1005: t3 = 8*t2
1006: t4 = t3-88
1007: a[t4] = 0.0
1008: j = j+1
1009: if j <= 10 goto 1003
1010: i = i+1
1011: if i <= 10 goto 1002
1012: i = 1
1013: t5 = i-1
1014: t6 = 88*t5
1015: a[t6] = 1.0
1016: i = i+1
1017: if i <= 11 goto 1013
\end{verbatim} \par}
\par\vspace{3pt}\noindent\textbf{Answer:} \rule{3.2cm}{0.4pt}
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.53} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[NAT]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries A computer has a memory hierarchy consisting of two-level cache (L1 and L2) and a main memory. If the processor needs to access data from memory, it first looks into L1 cache ($h_1 = 0.95$, access time $t_1 = 10\text{ ns}$). If not found in L1, it accesses L2 cache ($h_2 = 0.85$, access time including L1 miss penalty $t_2 = 20\text{ ns}$). If it misses in L2, it accesses main memory (access time including L1 and L2 miss penalties $t_{MM} = 200\text{ ns}$). The average memory access time in nanoseconds (ns) is \_\_\_\_\_\_\. (rounded off to two decimal places) \par}
\par\vspace{3pt}\noindent\textbf{Answer:} \rule{3.2cm}{0.4pt}
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.54} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[NAT]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries In optimal page replacement algorithm, information about all future page references is available to the operating system (OS). A modification of the optimal page replacement algorithm is as follows:
The OS correctly predicts only up to next 4 page references (including the current page) at the time of allocating a frame to a page.
A process accesses the pages in the following order of page numbers:
1, 3, 2, 4, 2, 3, 1, 2, 4, 3, 1, 4.
If the system has three memory frames that are initially empty, the number of page faults that will occur during execution of the process is \_\_\_\_\_\_\. (Answer in integer) \par}
\par\vspace{3pt}\noindent\textbf{Answer:} \rule{3.2cm}{0.4pt}
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.56} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[NAT]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Suppose a 5-bit message is transmitted from a source to a destination through a noisy channel. The probability that a bit of the message gets flipped during transmission is 0.01. Flipping of each bit is independent of one another. The probability that the message is delivered error-free to the destination is \_\_\_\_\_\_\. (rounded off to three decimal places) \par}
\par\vspace{3pt}\noindent\textbf{Answer:} \rule{3.2cm}{0.4pt}
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.57} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[NAT]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Suppose a message of size 15000 bytes is transmitted from a source to a destination using IPv4 protocol via two routers as shown: Source $\to$ Router-1 ($\text{MTU} = 5000$ bytes) $\to$ Router-2 ($\text{MTU} = 3000$ bytes) $\to$ Destination. Each router has a defined maximum transmission unit (MTU) including the 20-byte IP header. The number of fragments that will be delivered to the destination is \_\_\_\_\_\_\. (Answer in integer) \par}
\par\vspace{3pt}\noindent\textbf{Answer:} \rule{3.2cm}{0.4pt}
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.58} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[NAT]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Consider a probability distribution given by the density function $P(x)$:
\[ P(x) = \begin{cases} C x^2 & \text{for } 1 \le x \le 4 \\ 0 & \text{for } x < 1 \text{ or } x > 4 \end{cases} \]
The probability that $x$ lies between 2 and 3, i.e., $P(2 \le x \le 3)$ is \_\_\_\_\_\_\. (rounded off to three decimal places) \par}
\par\vspace{3pt}\noindent\textbf{Answer:} \rule{3.2cm}{0.4pt}
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.59} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[NAT]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Consider a finite state machine (FSM) with one input $X$ and one output $f$, represented by the given state transition table:
\par\smallskip
\begin{tabular}{|c|cc|cc|}
\hline
\textbf{Present state} & \multicolumn{2}{c|}{\textbf{Next state}} & \multicolumn{2}{c|}{\textbf{Output } $f$} \\
& $X=0$ & $X=1$ & $X=0$ & $X=1$ \\
\hline
A & F & B & 0 & 0 \\
B & D & C & 0 & 0 \\
C & F & E & 0 & 0 \\
D & G & A & 1 & 0 \\
E & D & C & 0 & 0 \\
F & F & B & 1 & 1 \\
G & G & H & 0 & 1 \\
H & G & A & 1 & 0 \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\par\smallskip
The minimum number of states required to realize this FSM is \_\_\_\_\_\_\. (Answer in integer) \par}
\par\vspace{3pt}\noindent\textbf{Answer:} \rule{3.2cm}{0.4pt}
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}

\par\vspace{2pt}

\par\vspace{3pt}\noindent\textbf{Answer:} \rule{3.2cm}{0.4pt}
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.61} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[NAT]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Consider the following C program:
\par\smallskip
\begin{verbatim}
#include <stdio.h>
int foo(int S[], int size){
    if(size == 0) return 0;
    if(size == 1) return 1;
    if(S[0] != S[1]) return 1 + foo(S+1, size-1);
    return foo(S+1, size-1);
}
int main(){
    int A[] = {0, 1, 2, 2, 2, 0, 0, 1, 1};
    printf("%d", foo(A, 9));
    return 0;
}
\end{verbatim}
The value printed by the given C program is \_\_\_\_\_\_\. (Answer in integer) \par}
\par\vspace{3pt}\noindent\textbf{Answer:} \rule{3.2cm}{0.4pt}
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.62} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[NAT]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Let LIST be a datatype for an implementation of linked list defined as follows:
\begin{verbatim}
typedef struct list {
    int data;
    struct list *next;
} LIST;
\end{verbatim}
Suppose a program has created two linked lists, L1 and L2, whose contents are given (code for creating L1 and L2 is not provided here). L1 contains 9 nodes, and L2 contains 7 nodes.
Consider the following C program segment that modifies the list L1. The number of nodes that will be there in L1 after the execution of the code segment is \_\_\_\_\_\_\. (Answer in integer)
\par\smallskip
\begin{verbatim}
int find (int query, LIST *list) {
    while (list != NULL){
        if(list->data == query) return 1;
        list = list->next;
    }
    return 0;
}
int main () {
    ...
    ptr1 = L1; ptr2 = L2;
    while (ptr1->next != NULL){
        query = ptr1->next->data;
        if (find (query, L2))
            ptr1->next = ptr1->next->next;
        else ptr1 = ptr1->next;
    }
    ...
    return 0;
}
\end{verbatim} \par}
\par\vspace{3pt}\noindent\textbf{Answer:} \rule{3.2cm}{0.4pt}
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.63} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[NAT]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Consider the following C program:
\par\smallskip
\begin{verbatim}
#include <stdio.h>
int gate (int n) {
    int d, t, newnum, turn;
    newnum = turn = 0; t = 1;
    while (n >= t) t *= 10;
    t /= 10;
    while (t > 0) {
        d = n / t;
        n = n % t;
        t /= 10;
        if (turn) newnum = 10 * newnum + d;
        turn = (turn + 1) % 2;
    }
    return newnum;
}
int main () {
    printf("%d", gate(14362));
    return 0;
}
\end{verbatim}
The value printed by the given C program is \_\_\_\_\_\_\. (Answer in integer) \par}
\par\vspace{3pt}\noindent\textbf{Answer:} \rule{3.2cm}{0.4pt}
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}

{\bfseries In a double hashing scheme, $h_1(k) = k \bmod 11$ and $h_2(k) = 1 + (k \bmod 7)$ are the auxiliary hash functions. The size $m$ of the hash table is 11. The hash function for the $i$-th probe in the open address table is $[h_1(k) + i \cdot h_2(k)] \bmod m$. The following keys are inserted in the given order: 63, 50, 25, 79, 67, 24.
The slot at which key 24 gets stored is \_\_\_\_\_\_\. (Answer in integer) \par}

\end{tabular*}

Select the correct option to complete the analogy. \par}

\noindent\textbf{Q.3} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries If $P e^x = Q e^{-x}$ for all real values of $x$, which one of the following statements is true? \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular*}{\columnwidth}{@{\extracolsep{\fill}}p{0.46\columnwidth}p{0.46\columnwidth}@{}}
\textbf{(A)}~$P = Q = 0$ & \textbf{(B)}~$P = Q = 1$ \\
\addlinespace[2pt]
\textbf{(C)}~$P = 1; Q = -1$ & \textbf{(D)}~$\frac{P}{Q} = 0$
\end{tabular*}
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.5} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Let $p_1$ and $p_2$ denote two arbitrary prime numbers. Which one of the following statements is correct for all values of $p_1$ and $p_2$? \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(A)}~$p_1 + p_2$ is not a prime number.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(B)}~$p_1 p_2$ is not a prime number.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(C)}~$p_1 + p_2 + 1$ is a prime number.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(D)}~$p_1 p_2 + 1$ is a prime number.
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}

\vspace{6pt}
\noindent{\footnotesize\textit{[Section Block: 2-mark questions]}}
\vspace{3pt}

\noindent\textbf{Q.6} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Based only on the conversation below, identify the logically correct inference:
``Even if I had known that you were in the hospital, I would not have gone there to see you'', Ramya told Josephine. \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(A)}~Ramya knew that Josephine was in the hospital.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(B)}~Ramya did not know that Josephine was in the hospital.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(C)}~Ramya and Josephine were once close friends; but now, they are not.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(D)}~Josephine was in the hospital due to an injury to her leg.
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.7} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries If $\text{IMAGE}$ and $\text{FIELD}$ are coded as $\text{FHBNJ}$ and $\text{EMFJG}$ respectively then, which one among the given options is the most appropriate code for $\text{BEACH}$? \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular*}{\columnwidth}{@{\extracolsep{\fill}}p{0.46\columnwidth}p{0.46\columnwidth}@{}}
\textbf{(A)}~CEADP & \textbf{(B)}~IDBFC \\
\addlinespace[2pt]
\textbf{(C)}~JGIBC & \textbf{(D)}~IBCEC
\end{tabular*}
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.8} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Which one of the following options is correct for the given data in the table?
\par\smallskip
\begin{center}\footnotesize
\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
\begin{tabular}{|c|c|c|c|}
\hline
\textbf{Iteration ($i$)} & \textbf{Input ($I$)} & \textbf{Output ($X$)} & \textbf{Output ($Y$)} \\ \hline\hline
0 & 20 & 20 & 20 \\ \hline
1 & $-4$ & 16 & $-80$ \\ \hline
2 & 10 & 26 & $-800$ \\ \hline
3 & 15 & 41 & $-12000$ \\ \hline
\end{tabular}\%
}\end{center} \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(A)}~$X(i) = X(i-1) + I(i); \; Y(i) = Y(i-1) I(i); \; i > 0$\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(B)}~$X(i) = X(i-1) I(i); \; Y(i) = Y(i-1) + I(i); \; i > 0$\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(C)}~$X(i) = X(i-1) I(i); \; Y(i) = Y(i-1) I(i); \; i > 0$\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(D)}~$X(i) = X(i-1) + I(i); \; Y(i) = Y(i-1) I(i-1); \; i > 0$
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}

\vspace{8pt}
\noindent\textbf{\large\underline{COMPUTER SCIENCE AND INFORMATION TECHNOLOGY}}\\[2pt]
\noindent{\footnotesize\textit{[Section Block: 1-mark questions]}}

\noindent\textbf{Q.11} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}

\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent

\textbf{(B)}~$\begin{pmatrix} 125 & 0 \\ 0 & 125 \end{pmatrix}$\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(C)}~$\begin{pmatrix} 625 & 0 \\ 0 & 625 \end{pmatrix}$\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(D)}~$\begin{pmatrix} 3125 & 0 \\ 0 & 3125 \end{pmatrix}$

\noindent\textbf{Q.13} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Consider a binary tree $T$ in which every node has either zero or two children. Let $n > 0$ be the number of nodes in $T$. Which ONE of the following is the number of nodes in $T$ that have exactly two children? \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular*}{\columnwidth}{@{\extracolsep{\fill}}p{0.46\columnwidth}p{0.46\columnwidth}@{}}
\textbf{(A)}~$\frac{n-2}{2}$ & \textbf{(B)}~$\frac{n-1}{2}$ \\
\addlinespace[2pt]
\textbf{(C)}~$\frac{n}{2}$ & \textbf{(D)}~$\frac{n+1}{2}$
\end{tabular*}
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.14} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}

\end{tabular*}
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.15} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Let $P(x)$ be an arbitrary predicate over the domain of natural numbers. Which ONE of the following statements is TRUE? \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(A)}~$(P(0) \land (\forall x [P(x) \implies P(x+1)])) \implies (\forall x P(x))$\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(B)}~$(P(0) \land (\forall x [P(x) \implies P(x-1)])) \implies (\forall x P(x))$\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(C)}~$(P(1000) \land (\forall x [P(x) \implies P(x-1)])) \implies (\forall x P(x))$\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(D)}~$(P(1000) \land (\forall x [P(x) \implies P(x+1)])) \implies (\forall x P(x))$
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.16} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Consider the following statements:
(i) Address Resolution Protocol (ARP) provides a mapping from an IP address to the corresponding hardware (link-layer) address.
(ii) A single TCP segment from a sender S to a receiver R cannot carry both data from S to R and acknowledgement for a segment from R to S.
Which ONE of the following is CORRECT? \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(A)}~Both (i) and (ii) are TRUE\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(B)}~(i) is TRUE and (ii) is FALSE\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(C)}~(i) is FALSE and (ii) is TRUE\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(D)}~Both (i) and (ii) are FALSE
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.17} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Consider the routing protocols given in List I and the names given in List II:
\par\smallskip
\begin{center}\footnotesize
\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|}
\hline
\textbf{List I} & \textbf{List II} \\ \hline\hline
(i) Distance vector routing & (a) Bellman-Ford \\ \hline
(ii) Link state routing & (b) Dijkstra \\ \hline
\end{tabular}\%
}\end{center}
\par\smallskip
For matching of items in List I with those in List II, which ONE of the following options is CORRECT? \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(A)}~(i) -- (a) and (ii) -- (b)\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(B)}~(i) -- (a) and (ii) -- (a)\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(C)}~(i) -- (b) and (ii) -- (a)\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(D)}~(i) -- (b) and (ii) -- (b)
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.18} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}

\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent

\textbf{(B)}~Internet Group Management Protocol (IGMP)\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(C)}~Open Shortest Path First (OSPF)\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent

\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.19} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Consider the following C program:
 \par}\begin{lstlisting}[language=C, basicstyle=\scriptsize\ttfamily, frame=single, rulecolor=\color{black!40}, tabsize=2, breaklines=true, aboveskip=4pt, belowskip=4pt]
#include <stdio.h>
void stringcopy(char *, char *);
int main() {
    char a[30] = "@#Hello World!";
    stringcopy(a, a + 2);
    printf("%s\n", a);
    return 0;
}
void stringcopy(char *s, char *t) {
    while(*t)
        *s++ = *t++;
}
\end{lstlisting}{\bfseries 
Which ONE of the following will be the output of the program? \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular*}{\columnwidth}{@{\extracolsep{\fill}}p{0.46\columnwidth}p{0.46\columnwidth}@{}}
\textbf{(A)}~@\#Hello World! & \textbf{(B)}~Hello World! \\
\addlinespace[2pt]
\textbf{(C)}~ello World! & \textbf{(D)}~Hello World!d!
\end{tabular*}
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.20} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Consider an unordered list of $N$ distinct integers. What is the minimum number of element comparisons required to find an integer in the list that is NOT the largest in the list? \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular*}{\columnwidth}{@{\extracolsep{\fill}}p{0.46\columnwidth}p{0.46\columnwidth}@{}}
\textbf{(A)}~1 & \textbf{(B)}~$N - 1$ \\
\addlinespace[2pt]
\textbf{(C)}~$N$ & \textbf{(D)}~$2N - 1$
\end{tabular*}
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.21} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Consider the following statements about the use of backpatching in a compiler for intermediate code generation:
(I) Backpatching can be used to generate code for Boolean expression in one pass.
(II) Backpatching can be used to generate code for flow-of-control statements in one pass.
Which ONE of the following options is CORRECT? \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(A)}~Only (I) is correct.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(B)}~Only (II) is correct.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(C)}~Both (I) and (II) are correct.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(D)}~Neither (I) nor (II) is correct.
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.22} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Given the following syntax directed translation rules:
- Rule 1: $R \to AB \;\{ B.i = R.i - 1; \; A.i = B.i; \; R.i = A.i + 1; \}$
- Rule 2: $P \to CD \;\{ P.i = C.i + D.i; \; D.i = C.i + 2; \}$
- Rule 3: $Q \to EF \;\{ Q.i = E.i + F.i; \}$
\par\smallskip
Which ONE is the CORRECT option among the following? \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(A)}~Rule 1 is S-attributed and L-attributed; Rule 2 is S-attributed and not L-attributed; Rule 3 is neither S-attributed nor L-attributed\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(B)}~Rule 1 is neither S-attributed nor L-attributed; Rule 2 is S-attributed and L-attributed; Rule 3 is S-attributed and L-attributed\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(C)}~Rule 1 is neither S-attributed nor L-attributed; Rule 2 is not S-attributed and is L-attributed; Rule 3 is S-attributed and L-attributed\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(D)}~Rule 1 is S-attributed and not L-attributed; Rule 2 is not S-attributed and is L-attributed; Rule 3 is S-attributed and L-attributed
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.23} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Consider a network that uses Ethernet and IPv4. Assume that IPv4 headers do not use any options field. Each Ethernet frame can carry a maximum of 1500 bytes in its data field. A UDP segment is transmitted. The payload (data) in the UDP segment is 7488 bytes.
Which ONE of the following choices has the CORRECT total number of fragments transmitted and the size of the last fragment including IPv4 header? \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(A)}~5 fragments, 1488 bytes\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(B)}~6 fragments, 88 bytes\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(C)}~6 fragments, 108 bytes\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(D)}~6 fragments, 116 bytes
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.24} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}

\textbf{(C)}~Any language accepted by a non-deterministic pushdown automaton.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(D)}~Any decidable language.
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}

\textbf{(A)}~Is $L(G_1) = L(G_2)$?\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(B)}~Is $L(G_1) \cap L(G_2) = \emptyset$?\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(C)}~Is $L(G_1) = L(R)$?\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(D)}~Is $L(G_1) = \emptyset$?
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}

\noindent\textbf{Q.27} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MSQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries An audit of a banking transactions system has found that on an earlier occasion, two joint holders of account $A$ attempted simultaneous transfers of Rs. 10000 each from account $A$ to account $B$. Which of the following properties is/are certain to have been violated by the system? \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular*}{\columnwidth}{@{\extracolsep{\fill}}p{0.46\columnwidth}p{0.46\columnwidth}@{}}

\textbf{(C)}~Isolation & \textbf{(D)}~Durability
\end{tabular*}

\textbf{(C)}~If $(u, v)$ is a non-tree edge of $G$ with respect to a BFS tree, then the distances from the source vertex $s$ to $u$ and $v$ in the BFS tree are within $\pm 1$ of each other.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent

\noindent\textbf{Q.37} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Let $G$ be an edge-weighted undirected graph with positive edge weights. Suppose a positive constant $\alpha$ is added to the weight of every edge in $G$. Which ONE of the following statements is TRUE? \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(A)}~Every MST remains an MST, and every SP remains an SP.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(B)}~MSTs need not remain MSTs, and every SP remains an SP.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(C)}~Every MST remains an MST, and SPs need not remain SPs.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(D)}~MSTs need not remain MSTs, and SPs need not remain SPs.
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.38} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries A meld operation on two instances of a data structure combines them into one single instance of the same data structure. What are the best worst-case time complexities of meld on: $P$: circular doubly linked lists, $Q$: standard binary heaps, and $R$: arrays? \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(A)}~P: $\Theta(1)$, Q: $\Theta(n)$, R: $\Theta(n)$\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(B)}~P: $\Theta(1)$, Q: $\Theta(n\log n)$, R: $\Theta(n)$\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(C)}~P: $\Theta(n)$, Q: $\Theta(n\log n)$, R: $\Theta(n^2)$\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(D)}~P: $\Theta(1)$, Q: $\Theta(n)$, R: $\Theta(n\log n)$
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}

\noindent\textbf{Q.40} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}

\textbf{(B)}~$G$ is CLR(1), not LALR(1)\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(C)}~$G$ is LALR(1), not SLR(1)\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent

\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.41} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MCQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries An array $A$ of length $n$ with distinct elements is said to be bitonic if there is an index $1 \le i \le n$ such that $A[1..i]$ is strictly increasing and $A[i..n]$ is strictly decreasing. What is the optimal time complexity to search for a key in a bitonic array? \par}

\begin{tabular*}{\columnwidth}{@{\extracolsep{\fill}}p{0.46\columnwidth}p{0.46\columnwidth}@{}}

\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.42} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MSQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Let $\mathcal{F}$ be the set of all functions from $\{1, \dots, n\}$ to $\{0, 1\}$. Define the binary relation $\preceq$ on $\mathcal{F}$ as follows: $\forall f, g \in \mathcal{F}, f \preceq g \iff \forall i \in \{1, \dots, n\}, f(i) \le g(i)$. Which of the following is/are TRUE? \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(A)}~$\preceq$ is a symmetric relation\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(B)}~$(\mathcal{F}, \preceq)$ is a partial order\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(C)}~$(\mathcal{F}, \preceq)$ is a lattice\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(D)}~$\preceq$ is an equivalence relation
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.43} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MSQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Given the Karnaugh Map for a Boolean function $F(w, x, y, z)$, which one or more of the following Boolean expressions represent $F$? \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(A)}~$\overline{w}\overline{x}\overline{y}\overline{z} + w\overline{x}\overline{y}\overline{z} + \overline{w}\overline{x}y\overline{z} + w\overline{x}y\overline{z} + xz$\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(B)}~$\overline{w}\overline{x}\overline{y}\overline{z} + \overline{w}\overline{x}y\overline{z} + w\overline{x}yz + xz$\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(C)}~$\overline{w}\overline{x}\overline{y}\overline{z} + w\overline{x}\overline{y}\overline{z} + w\overline{x}\overline{y}z + xz$\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(D)}~$\overline{x}\overline{z} + xz$
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.44} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MSQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Consider a system of linear equations $PX = Q$ where $P \in \mathbb{R}^{3 \times 3}$ and $Q \in \mathbb{R}^{3 \times 1}$. Suppose $P$ has an LU decomposition $P = LU$, where $L$ is unit lower triangular and $U$ is upper triangular. Which of the following statements is/are TRUE? \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(A)}~The system $PX = Q$ can be solved by first solving $LY = Q$ and then $UX = Y$.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(B)}~If $P$ is invertible, then both $L$ and $U$ are invertible.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(C)}~If $P$ is singular, then at least one of the diagonal elements of $U$ is zero.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(D)}~If $P$ is symmetric, then both $L$ and $U$ are symmetric.
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.45} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MSQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Consider a stack data structure into which we can PUSH and POP records. Assume that each record has a numeric key. Which of the following strategies allows finding the record with the minimum key in $O(1)$ worst-case time without increasing the asymptotic time for PUSH and POP? \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(A)}~Keep with every record in the stack, a pointer to the record with the smallest key below it.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(B)}~Keep a pointer to the record with the smallest key in the stack.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(C)}~Keep an auxiliary array in which the key values of the records in the stack are maintained in sorted order.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(D)}~Keep a Min-Heap in which the key values of the records in the stack are maintained.
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.46} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MSQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}

$R_1(A, B, C, D)$ with FDs, and $R_2(E, F, G, H)$ with FDs.

\noindent\textbf{Q.47} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MSQ]}}

\textbf{(A)}~Least Recently Used page replacement policy\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent

\noindent\textbf{Q.48} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MSQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Let $P = \{P_1, P_2, P_3, P_4\}$ be active processes and $R = \{R_1, R_2, R_3, R_4\}$ be resources in an operating system. Given the allocation and request graph, which of the following actions makes the system deadlock free? \par}

\textbf{(B)}~Aborting $P_3$ makes the system deadlock free.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent

\noindent\textbf{Q.49} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MSQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Three floating point numbers $X, Y,$ and $Z$ are stored in three registers RX, RY, and RZ, respectively in IEEE 754 single-precision format. Which of the following relationships is/are CORRECT? \par}

\begin{tabular*}{\columnwidth}{@{\extracolsep{\fill}}p{0.46\columnwidth}p{0.46\columnwidth}@{}}

\noindent\textbf{Q.50} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MSQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Which of the following Boolean algebraic equation(s) is/are CORRECT? \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(A)}~$\overline{A}BC + A\overline{B}\overline{C} + \overline{A}\overline{B}\overline{C} + A\overline{B}C + ABC = BC + \overline{B}\overline{C} + \overline{A}\overline{B}$\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(B)}~$AB + \overline{A}C + BC = AB + \overline{A}C$\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(C)}~$(A + C)(\overline{A} + B) = AB + \overline{A}C$\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent

\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.51} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MSQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Consider two grammars $G_1$ and $G_2$ with production rules for dangling-else conditional statements. Which of the following statements is/are CORRECT? \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(A)}~$G_1$ is not $LL(1)$ and $G_2$ is $LL(1)$.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(B)}~$G_1$ is $LL(1)$ and $G_2$ is not $LL(1)$.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(C)}~$G_1$ and $G_2$ are not $LL(1)$.\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(D)}~$G_1$ and $G_2$ are ambiguous.
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.52} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MSQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Let $\Sigma = \{a, b, c\}$. For $x \in \Sigma^*$, let $\#_\alpha(x)$ denote the number of occurrences of $\alpha$ in $x$. Which one or more of the following languages is/are regular? \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(A)}~$\{a^m b^n \mid m, n \ge 0\}$\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(B)}~$\{a, b\}^* \cap \{a^m b^n c^{m-n} \mid m \ge n \ge 0\}$\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(C)}~$\{w \in \{a, b\}^* \mid \#_a(w) \equiv 2 \pmod 7 \text{ and } \#_b(w) \equiv 3 \pmod 9\}$\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(D)}~$\{w \in \{a, b\}^* \mid \#_a(w) \equiv 2 \pmod 7 \text{ and } \#_a(w) = \#_b(w)\}$
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.54} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[MSQ]}}
\par\vspace{2pt}
{\bfseries Consider the relational schema:
- $\text{Students}(\underline{\text{rollno}}, \text{name}, \text{age}, \text{cgpa})$
- $\text{Enrolled}(\underline{\text{rollno}, \text{courseno}})$
\par\smallskip
Which of the following SQL query/queries correctly find(s) the name of all students who have enrolled in course 1470? \par}
\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(A)}~SELECT S.name \newline FROM Students S \newline WHERE EXISTS (SELECT * FROM Enrolled E WHERE E.courseno = 1470 AND E.rollno = S.rollno);\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(B)}~SELECT S.name \newline FROM Students S \newline WHERE SIZEOF (SELECT * FROM Enrolled E WHERE E.courseno = 1470 AND E.rollno = S.rollno) > 0;\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(C)}~SELECT S.name \newline FROM Students S \newline WHERE 0 < (SELECT COUNT(*) FROM Enrolled E WHERE E.courseno = 1470 AND E.rollno = S.rollno);\par\vspace{2pt}\noindent
\textbf{(D)}~SELECT S.name \newline FROM Students S NATURAL JOIN Enrolled E \newline WHERE E.courseno = 1470;
\par\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.25pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent\textbf{Q.55} \hfill {\footnotesize\textsf{[NAT]}}

The first level (L1) cache access time is 1 ns with 0.8 hit rate, L2 access time is 4 ns with 0.9 hit rate, and main memory access time is 50 ns. The average memory access time in nanoseconds is \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_. (Answer in decimal) \par}
